- My **Ne4** and **Bg5** are beautifully positioned

**Key Tactic: Bxd2!**

After `Bxd2`:
- **Kxd2 is ILLEGAL** — my **Ne4** controls d2, so the king can't capture
- If White plays the natural recapture **2.Bxd2**, I follow with **2...Nxd2** creating a devastating **fork on Rb1 and Bf1** — White loses a piece!
- If White avoids the trap with **2.Qxd5** (best defense), I can recapture with **2...Bxc1 3.Rxc1** and maintain my advantage

This move exploits the geometric relationship between my knight on e4 (defending d2) and bishop on g5 (capturing on d2). White must choose between falling into the fork trap or accepting a weaker position.

**Action:** `make_move g5d2`

After winning the knight in the main line, I'd be **up a full knight plus 2 pawns** — a completely winning position.

**My move:**

**e5 is completely undefended.** Also, no white piece can recapture on e5 afterward.

I also checked alternatives:
- `Nxf2+` wins only a pawn (King just steps to c2)
- No mate-in-1 exists (Bg4→d1 blocked by Be2, Qd8→d1 blocked by the d5 pawn)

So `Rxe5` wins the queen outright — a free queen for nothing, since after the capture White has no way to take the rook back.

2. **My advantages**: I have a strong pin on d2 (my Bb4 pins the pawn against White's king on e1) and a relative pin on Nf3 against the Rh1 (my Qd5 eyes through e4-f3-g2-h1), keeping White's pieces frozen.
3. **White's problems**: White's king is stuck in the center, Bc1 can only go to b2, Bf1 only to g2 — White is very cramped.

Retreating the bishop to e6 is the best choice: it centralizes the piece, keeps the queen's diagonal to h1 open (maintaining the pin on Nf3), adds pressure on White's g4 pawn (tying the h3 pawn to its defense), is well-defended by f7, and prepares to castle next move. Squares like d3 (exd3 wins a pawn for White), c2 (Qxc2 wins the bishop), and g4 (hxg4) all lose material.

- White's Bg2 is currently restricted by its own Nf3 on the long diagonal.

**Decision — 1...e4!:** Strike now with tempo while White's king is still on e1:
1. **Gains space** and cramps White's position, seizing the initiative.
2. **Kicks the Nf3** to an awkward square (Ne1/Ng2/Nh2 are passive; Ne5?? loses to Nxe5, Nd4?? loses to Nxd4/Qxd4, Nxf7?? loses to Kxf7, and Ng5 is answered by h6! forcing Nxe6 or material loss).
3. **Blocks White's Bg2** on the long diagonal (the e4-pawn sits on the light-square diagonal g2–b8), neutralizing White's best minor piece.
4. The pawn is tactically secure — nothing in White's camp attacks e4 — and it's over-defended potential threats are covered (Qd5 and Nf6 both eye e4 if White later plays d3/f3).

This deprives White of the comfortable O-O, d3, e4 setup and lets me castle next move with a clear space advantage.
